# Changelog

## 1.169.0

**`/ship` reported a clean deploy while a breached go-live obligation sat in production, and following the step exactly is what produced that answer.** Step 4's task-doc discovery builds its search from the files in the current diff, so it can only ever surface obligations the current ship created. The one that mattered belonged to a domain with zero files in the diff: a seeder wired into neither the app's seed entry point nor either deploy workflow, whose table had shipped empty on an *earlier* deploy. Structurally invisible to a keyword search derived from this diff, and a spot-check of live records showed everything healthy because every existing record predated the feature and rendered through a legacy path. The next record created would have generated with the seeded content missing entirely. Step 4 now sweeps `tasks/**/current.md` for manual-action vocabulary independent of the diff, on the reasoning that the deploy exposing a standing obligation is rarely the deploy that created it.

Two traps from the same incident are now written down. A doc saying a feature is "not yet on prod" is a claim to check against branch history (`git merge-base --is-ancestor`), not a fact to route by — the doc was written when it was true and never edited after the merge that made it false. And verifying this class of obligation by testing that a table *exists* proves nothing: a migrated-but-unseeded table passes that guard, so the code takes its normal path over an empty set and degrades silently rather than erroring. The recipe moved to `ship/references/ship-deploy-verification.md` as a new **Seeded Reference Data** artifact type, including why guessing the version identifier returns zero too and reads as confirmation. Net effect on `ship/SKILL.md`: 161 → 159 lines, two paragraphs added on the hot path and three relocated to the cold-path reference.

`/commit` gained one clause for the adjacent miss: a staged task doc from a domain the code didn't touch gets written off as riding along, which is safe when the doc records *build* state and wrong when it records *live* state (fixtures bought on production, a flag set on one environment). The latter is a standing fact about the running system and survives into whatever ships next.

## 1.168.0

**Two dead `📖` pointers fixed, and a check added so the next one gets caught.** A pointer's relative depth differs by where the citing file sits — `../_shared/…` is right in a `SKILL.md` and wrong one directory down in `references/*.md`, where it resolves to a folder that doesn't exist. The two forms are the same string, so reading a pointer can't tell them apart, and a wrong-depth one passes every other check: the target file exists, the prose around it is complete, nothing is orphaned. It just silently goes nowhere. Both instances found are fixed (`haiku/references/verifying.md`, `update-plugin/references/routing-gotchas.md` — the latter had been dead since 2026-08-09). `_shared/references/editing-skills-checklist.md` now says to resolve a pointer by `ls`-ing its target from the citing file's own directory rather than by eye, and to sweep the pattern rather than fix one instance.

Also in this release: `setup-playwright`'s new mobile-viewport section moved from *Scaffolding a new suite* to *Hardening an existing suite*, since its trigger phrases ("our e2e never tests mobile") describe a suite you already have. If you asked about mobile coverage and got routed past it, that's why.

## 1.167.0

**`unhobble-instructions` and `haiku` were unhobbled, the former by running it on its own SKILL.md.** Both had accumulated the shape they exist to remove: `haiku`'s Verification section had grown one appended paragraph per session, each naming a distinct way a delegated report misleads, and `unhobble`'s own six-question read had drifted into a wall no reader would carry. `unhobble/SKILL.md` 26.7KB → 20.2KB, `haiku/SKILL.md` 16.9KB → 9KB resident with the symptom-indexed gotchas moved to a new `haiku/references/verifying.md`.

- **Running the skill on itself cost two live pointers.** The pass dropped `📖 references/target-types.md` — leaving a 25-line reference file with no path to it from anywhere — and the Anthropic six-shifts source the whole skill implements, including the note that two of the six are the ones a structural pass silently skips. Both patched back rather than reverting an otherwise sound 31% cut. This is the extraction-pointing-at-nothing failure the skill warns about, produced by the skill on itself, which is the argument for verifying a self-applied pass against a snapshot rather than against its own report.
- **The previous version's fix had introduced a duplicate.** 1.166.0 added the deletion-disguised-as-routing mechanism to both `unhobble/references/verifying.md` and `haiku/SKILL.md` in near-identical words. The two files' agents were told about the pair and asked to rule rather than edit across the boundary; `verifying.md` now owns it as a named principle and `haiku` carries the check without restating the mechanism.

## 1.166.0

**`unhobble-instructions` never said where a companion goes, so three parallel agents wrote theirs into the home directory.** The skill's move rule requires the destination to exist before content is cut, but "exists" is a claim about a path and the skill stated no path — a `~/.claude-companions/…` write while editing a project file lands outside the repo, where nothing will load it, and the source file reads as a clean extraction either way. The location rule already existed in `condense-claude-md/references/structural-splits.md`, two pointer-hops from the sentence a session is reading when it writes.

- **The fix went next to the write, not into another pointer.** A correct rule sitting behind a `📖` two hops away is a rule that doesn't fire; the path, the pointer-shape check (`ls` the path the pointer *states*, not the one you meant) and the global-`~/.claude` exception that makes the wrong path look plausible now sit in the move rule itself.
- **A companion that already exists is the more dangerous deletion.** Separately, one agent dropped ten domain sections citing companions the source already pointed at — no new file, pointer resolves, report says every anchor is fine. Topic-matching is why it looked safe: the companion carried the domain's general vocabulary and not the specific mechanism the deleted prose existed to state. Landed in `unhobble-instructions/references/verifying.md` and in `haiku`'s verification section, which already covered the never-written and dead-path cases but not the one that passes an existence check.

## 1.165.0

**A harness that asks you to narrate will walk you into `/done`'s no-prose rule.** Step 1 requires every review agent to go out in one message with no text before the calls, because narration ends the message and abandons the rest of the batch. That rule reads as though silence were the natural default — and for a background job or any status-tracked session it isn't: those system prompts ask for a line of approach *before* acting, so a session doing exactly what its harness told it lands on the violation. Hit in a real `/done` run whose batch happened to survive; it usually doesn't.

- **The fix is where the narration gets spent, not how the rule is worded.** The existing wording was already about as sharp as a rule gets (`non-negotiable`, mechanism spelled out), so escalating it further would have added length and changed nothing. Pay the convention on the message where the partition is settled, then let the next message carry only calls.
- **Landed in `_shared/references/explore-delegation.md`, not just `done`.** That reference owns the one-message batching rule and several skills restate it, so every skill that fans out agents inherits the collision note rather than only the one where it surfaced.

## 1.164.0

**`setup-playwright` now scaffolds mobile viewport coverage instead of desktop-only.** A suite built from this skill got a single Desktop Chrome project, which meant any layout that swaps below a breakpoint — a tab strip replacing a two-column grid, cards replacing a table — had no regression coverage at all. Nothing failed, so the gap was invisible: the suite reported green while never rendering the branch, and a mobile-only break would have shipped looking tested. Found by a user asking why a mobile layout was being verified by screenshot rather than by the suite.

- **The `grepInvert` half is the part that costs a red run to learn.** Tagging specs `@mobile` and adding `grep: /@mobile/` to a new mobile project looks complete and isn't — the desktop project still matches those same files and runs them at desktop width, where the mobile-only markup doesn't exist. They fail correctly, for the wrong reason, in the wrong project, and it reads as the new specs being broken. Both projects need the tag: `grep` on one, `grepInvert` on the other.
- **Opt-in tagging rather than a second full pass.** Most specs assert data, not layout; re-running all of them at a second viewport doubles the suite to re-check things that can't differ.
- **Verify by comparing per-project counts.** A tag typo produces a mobile project matching zero specs, which reports as a pass — the failure mode of the fix is the same silent green it was added to remove.

## 1.163.0

**Your answers will look different after this update.** syafiqkit now ships a session-start hook that shapes how every response is written: the thing to do comes first, multi-step work is numbered, time estimates use real units, and openers like "Great question" and closers like "Let me know if you need anything else" are gone. Update with `claude plugin update syafiqkit@syafiqkit`. It applies automatically when a session starts, resumes, is cleared or compacts, including sessions where you never invoke a syafiqkit skill; a session you *fork* is the one case it skips. **There is no setting to turn it off** — the only way off it today is `claude plugin uninstall syafiqkit@syafiqkit`, which removes the skills too. If it gets in your way, say so and it can be reworked or made optional in a follow-up.

- **The style applies whether or not a skill is involved.** A skill only reaches sessions that call it, so anything about how output should read had nowhere to live — spreading it across 33 skill files would drift immediately and still miss every skill added later. A `SessionStart` hook fires once before anything else, which makes it the only host that reaches a whole session.
- **What it actually changes**: answers lead with a command, path, or the answer itself rather than context; work spanning several steps arrives numbered, one action per step; estimates say "about fifteen minutes if the tests already cover this" instead of "some work"; a second issue found mid-task gets finished-then-offered rather than interleaved; errors state cause then fix without the preamble. Explanations still run as long as the topic needs — asking to be walked through something overrides the brevity, and so does anything requiring confirmation before a destructive action.
- **Windows without Git Bash gets nothing, silently.** The hook is a `cat` of a markdown file, which needs a POSIX-ish environment. On bare Windows it no-ops with no error and every other skill keeps working — you lose the output shaping, not the plugin.
- **`unhobble-instructions` now handles files that are delivered rather than opened.** Three of its four routing outcomes end in "move it behind a pointer," which silently stops working when the target is a hook payload or a runtime-assembled prompt: there's no working directory, so a `📖` line resolves nowhere. The routing collapses to keep-or-cut, and that raises the stakes on cutting — content that would normally be relocated gets deleted instead. Two passes over this release's own ruleset each cut a fact a cold reader couldn't derive, which is what the gap looks like in practice.

## 1.162.0

`haiku` now covers the target-file-type collision and the verification sweep that passes while a fact is inverted. Update with `claude plugin update syafiqkit@syafiqkit`. No regeneration needed — skills only, no agent templates changed. If a recent dispatch verified a rewrite by grepping for domain terms and finding them all present, that file is worth re-reading — the sweep would have returned clean either way.

- **A skill's required mechanic can have nowhere to land when the target isn't the file type it was built for.** `haiku` already covered a prompt constraint that forbids what the skill needs (banning writes breaks `unhobble-instructions`' companion split); the inverse went unnamed. A skill scoped to instruction-bearing markdown assumes a docs tree around it, so dispatched against a prompt string inside a `.ts` module, a template in a view directory, or a docstring in source, its extract-to-companion move has no destination a pointer can resolve to. The boundary is a fact about the target the agent can't derive, so it belongs in the prompt — but stated with its alternative ("stay inline or be cut on its merits") rather than as a bare prohibition, which would rebuild the trap the preceding paragraph exists to prevent. Measured rather than assumed: two bare dispatches against `.ts` prompt modules inlined their detail and created no companion, so the mechanic may simply not fire and the line is worth testing before treating it as required.
- **An all-present term sweep is the one verification result that invites you to stop looking.** The existing guidance covered greps producing false positives — reworded facts reading as deletions — and stopped there. The mirror case is worse precisely because it returns clean: a rewrite that preserves `net`, `gross` and every deduction label while inverting the arithmetic they sit in passes a presence check completely, and the agent reports full preservation because from its side nothing was dropped. A missing-term count at least sends you to the file; an all-present count reads as a verdict. Where a file states a rule the rest of the system depends on — a formula, a precedence order, a direction of comparison — that claim now gets read and re-derived against the original rather than confirmed by the presence of its nouns.

## 1.161.0

`plan-worklist` no longer offers an unqualified escape from its `read-summary` step, and `read-summary` states why a partial doc read misroutes tool selection later. Update with `claude plugin update syafiqkit@syafiqkit`. No regeneration needed — skills only, no agent templates changed. If a recent scoping pass answered an architecture question from docs read with `head`/`cat` rather than the skill, the layer it concluded is worth re-checking against something installed on disk.

- **"if it isn't already loaded" was read as permission to substitute a cheaper read, not as a test of whether one had happened.** `plan-worklist` step 1 delegated context loading to `read-summary` and gave the reason (a reviewer can't tell a deliberate scope-cut from a real gap without the doc's stated intent), but the leading conditional let a couple of `head -50` calls stand in for the pass. `agent-setup` had already reached the opposite conclusion for the same exposure — `Explore`/`Plan` run `read-summary` on *every* call because open-ended prompts have no natural changed-files gate — so the fix aligns the two: the condition is now satisfied only by a full pass for that domain in that session, and the step names what a truncated read drops (content-based discovery of which docs apply, `📖`/`Related:` pointers, whole-file reads).
- **A doc's architecture line is the fact a partial read is most likely to mangle, and the damage lands somewhere other than the doc.** Front matter and file tables name a technology in shorthand while the prose underneath says which surface of it the project uses — SDK or raw API, subprocess or library call, subscription credentials or a key. Stopping at the shorthand leaves the vocabulary intact and the model wrong, which then routes a later step into a reference scoped to a layer the code never touches; the answer that comes back is correct in general and false here, and nothing about it looks wrong. `read-summary` now says to read past the tables when architecture bears on the next decision, and to confirm the layer against an installed package, config file, or type definition before building on it.

## 1.160.0

`haiku` now checks whose work is in a file before snapshotting it, and `task-summary` states the routing step that has to happen before it collapses a Last Session. Update with `claude plugin update syafiqkit@syafiqkit`. No regeneration needed — skills only, no agent templates changed. If a recent batch dispatch ran against a tree with uncommitted work in it, the docs it "verified" are worth re-reading; the checks would have passed either way.

- **A snapshot taken before dispatch fixes a baseline without establishing whose baseline it is.** `haiku`'s pre-dispatch step was purely mechanical (`cp` the targets, record `wc -lc`), and the prompt-writing section above it opened with "against a clean tree" as an assumption rather than something to verify. Uncommitted edits already in a target get frozen in as though they were the committed state, and every downstream check then measures the agent against them — so an agent reporting pre-existing content as its own output verifies clean, and four agents that did nothing at all are indistinguishable from three that worked. The fix points at `_shared/references/diff-ownership.md`, which already said it applies "before any step that writes, partitions, or scopes agents across a working tree" and named three skills; the one skill that dispatches concurrent writers wasn't among them.
- **A wrong non-derivable fact in a dispatch prompt is more destructive than an over-specified one.** The existing guidance covers padding (wastes the judgement you dispatched for) and impossible constraints (the skill's correct behaviour becomes unreachable), but not a stated fact simply being false. A convention inferred from two files becomes "this project's format" one sentence later; the agent obeys, converts files that were already correct, and reports successful conformance. Measuring first is one classification loop over the target set — and where the tree turns out to have no house style, that inconsistency is itself the fact worth passing along.
- **`task-summary`'s "rewrite Last Session wholesale" carried its routing condition as a parenthetical, and the parenthetical is what got dropped.** The main clause mandates the collapse, so deleting reads as compliance while "move any still load-bearing facts into their typed sections first" reads as a gloss. It also sat in a standing-constraints paragraph above the numbered procedure, where a session walking steps never re-reads it. Routing is now stated as the first half of a two-step operation, with the note that older entries are *more* likely to be shipped behaviour rather than session narration — the opposite of how stale they look — and that grepping for each fact after the rewrite is what tells you which one had no other home.

## 1.159.0

`update-claude-docs` now checks `.gitignore` before routing a personal fact, instead of assuming `CLAUDE.local.md` is the only private file. No action needed from you — but if a recent session wrote a container name, server alias or machine-specific tool behaviour into a team-visible `CLAUDE.md`, it's worth moving.

- **Which files are private is a per-repo `.gitignore` fact, and the skill was treating it as a filename convention.** The routing ladder named `CLAUDE.local.md` as the private rung, so a session with a companions tree that splits `shared/` (tracked) from `local/` (ignored) had nothing telling it the second one existed. The misroute is invisible after the fact because the content is accurate — only its audience is wrong — so the fix is a `git check-ignore` probe at the moment the routing decision gets made, plus a note that `git ls-files` answers a different question and reports "untracked" for a new file that isn't ignored at all.
- **The `CLAUDE.local.md` checklist was five credential-shaped bullets, which is why infrastructure handles didn't read as belonging there.** Container names, server aliases and the behaviour of a tool only you have installed all have the shared system as their *subject*, so they pattern-match as team knowledge; what makes them personal is that nobody else can type them and have them work. That distinction is now its own entry rather than left to be inferred from "credentials/tokens".

## 1.158.0

`read-summary` now notices when a project's generated agents are behind their templates. No action needed from you — a session reading `read-summary` now checks this itself before dispatching a project agent, and tells you if a file is behind.

- **A constraint added to the templates reaches nobody until someone re-runs `/agent-setup`, and nothing was telling them to.** 1.154.0 closed a hole where a dispatched agent could hand your brief to a copy of itself, but the fix lands in `skills/agent-setup/templates/` while the file that actually governs behaviour is the `.claude/agents/*.md` copy generated in each project — possibly months earlier. A stale agent never announces itself: it accepts the dispatch and returns a normally-shaped report, so the only tell is that nobody looked. `/agent-setup` already does a thorough per-hunk drift comparison and keeps owning that; what was missing was any reason to invoke it, so the new check is a cheap one-line probe in the skill most sessions already run at the start.
- **A safety-relevant rule is the right thing to probe for, because it either appears in every current agent or in none.** `Explore` is excluded by name — nested `Explore` is its designed behaviour, and a check that flags a correct file trains its reader to ignore the output.
- **The snippet was wrong twice before it shipped, both times returning a confident inverted answer.** `grep -Ll` silently means `-l` on BSD grep, since the later flag wins — so the first version listed every compliant agent as non-compliant, and its control passed anyway, because a file matching neither pattern is absent from a match-list and a non-match list alike. The second version put `2>/dev/null` mid-`find`-expression, where it parses as a search path rather than a redirect. Both are now in the plugin `CLAUDE.md`'s snippet rule: a control has to be able to fail in the direction you're worried about, which for an inverting flag means a file on each side.

## 1.157.0

`setup-playwright` covered making a suite trustworthy and never mentioned recording it. A session recorded a tenancy suite to review the flows by eye and found four product defects the green suite had no way to see.

- **A recorded run is a UI review surface, not a debugging aid, and the skill treated it as neither.** Twenty-eight passing tests, and watching them surfaced raw `<span class="bold">` markup rendering as literal text in the clause editor, an unsubstituted `{{clause_ref:}}` token, a dialog rendering "The clauses below refer to ." with an empty name, and copy promising a renter an email that no code sends. Every one of those is invisible to assertions, because a spec asserts what someone thought to assert — the value is that a human watches the flows the suite already drives, so the marginal cost is a flag.
- **Video is a context-creation option, which is the entire difficulty.** `use.video` reaches only contexts the runner builds for its own `page` fixture; hand-built contexts (any multi-party flow holding two sessions open) need context-level `recordVideo`. Set one and half the suite silently records nothing while the config reads as done — and the two halves land in different directories, so the missing half reads as "that spec didn't record."
- **Encoding stops when a context closes, so a fast spec records its loading state and nothing else.** The run is green and the video looks broken, and that disagreement reads as a product defect rather than a capture artifact. Hold contexts open briefly behind the recording flag; a normal run pays nothing.
- **Naming is what makes twenty videos reviewable, and the obvious fallback destroys it.** A hand-built context's video lands under a content hash the runner reports nowhere, so collection numbers by creation time and every file becomes anonymous. The mapping *is* recoverable — from inside the test, while the page is still open, where the video handle resolves to its path. Captured live: 31 videos went from `context-07.webm` to `tenancy-flow-the-invited-witness-signs-through-the-public-token-link-witness.webm`. Routing every context through one close helper gives the tail wait and the naming a single home, so an unnamed video also flags a probably-truncated one.
- `uiux` gains the same fact from the reviewer's side — a project with an E2E suite has a third evidence source beside screenshots and a browser agent, and it is the cheapest. Both files carry the viewport caveat: a cut-off edge in a recording is the frame boundary until proven otherwise, or the review chases a layout bug that isn't there.

## 1.156.0

`task-summary` validated its own diff and called that a valid doc. Skill-only change, nothing to regenerate.

- **Every check in §5 was scoped to the write that had just happened, which is the one place staleness never is.** The three essential checks ask whether this edit lost a row, broke a structure, or duplicated a phrase; the additional ones are prefixed *"run these if they apply to your write."* So a doc passes validation cleanly while carrying a paragraph that went false three sessions ago — nothing in the write touched it, so nothing read it. The user's report was that docs are stale "almost every time," and the skill was working as written the whole time.
- **The sessions that create staleness and the sessions that could catch it are structurally different sessions.** Rename a set of containers and you update the doc describing the rename, thoroughly and correctly. The sibling `decisions/*.md` mentioning the old name in passing is not in your diff, not in §1's scan (which maps *code* changes to docs, not doc changes to sibling docs), and not in §5's scope — three independent misses, none of which look like a miss. Observed live: a decisions file opened for unrelated work still claimed production "runs under the historical `ar_standby_*` container names," falsified by a rename the previous session had itself performed and documented.
- **`read-summary` already carried the rule; the writing skill didn't.** *"Reading a doc is also auditing it"* has sat in the reading skill's §What Task Docs Are, naming the fields that decay most — but that skill's job ends before anyone has the file open with edit intent. The fix is one paragraph at the top of §5 reframing what "true" covers, placed above the three checks so it's read before them rather than appended as a fourth. Deliberately not duplicated into `/done` and `/quick-done`'s exit gates: both invoke `task-summary`, so the rule fires through them, and a gate row asserting it separately would be the same fact in three files drifting apart.
- Includes the honesty clause the symmetric failure needs — where the doc contradicts what the session observed, verify before flipping, since a doc claim is as often right as it is stale and an unverified "correction" discredits a source that was correct.

## 1.155.0

`/haiku`'s verification section had a branch that let a grep authorise the one irreversible action it offers. Skill-only change, nothing to regenerate.

- **"The damage is systemic, revert to the snapshot" was reachable without ever reading the file.** Every symptom listed as qualifying — a dead pointer, a missing section, a byte count contradicting the report — is one a grep surfaces on its own, so finding one read as pre-authorisation and the "read it whole" instruction sixteen lines above became a formality already earned past. In a live session a `CLAUDE.local.md` rewrite was overwritten from snapshot on six zero-hit probes against a claimed pointer target; the rewrite turned out to be good, the user had to say so twice, and it was restored unread. The symptoms establish that something is wrong, never that the rest is worthless — the constraint now sits directly under the branch rather than upstream of it, and one duplicate `**Tell:**` was folded into the sharper existing one.
- **A user vouching for delegated output ends the verification for the whole batch, not the file under discussion.** Told "I don't care, I checked it, it's very good," the session narrowed that to the one target named in the surrounding turn and kept auditing the other four — which reads as compliance while ignoring what was meant. Verification exists to tell the user whether to trust the result; once they've read it themselves, further findings are second-guessing a judgement already made. A defect already in hand gets stated in one line and left with them.
- **A partitioning constraint silently disabled the dispatched skill, and it looked like the agents misbehaving.** Prompts for a five-file batch carried *edit only that file, do not move content into the others* — correct for keeping concurrent agents off shared bytes, and fatal to `unhobble-instructions`, which moves detail into companions and requires writing the destination before cutting the source. Banned from creating files, cutting was the only move left, so three passes returned leaner files pointing at companions that were never written, reported as moves. The rule they appeared to violate had been made unreachable by the prompt. `/haiku` now says to read a prohibition against what the skill actually does, and to name contested paths rather than forbidding writes in general.
- **Verify a claimed MOVE by opening the destination, resolving the pointer from the source file's own directory.** Two failures look identical from the source: never written, and written where nothing resolves — a sibling `.claude-companions/` one level up from the real one is the shape it took. Checking from the wrong cwd is its own trap, reporting every companion missing and inviting a "fix" to correct paths.

## 1.154.0

Agents that can dispatch are now told what they may dispatch. Regenerate project agents, or apply the one-line constraint by hand.

- **Seven templates granted the `Agent` tool; five carried only a YAML comment.** `- Agent  # lets this agent spawn Explore agents` is documentation for whoever edits the file and reaches the agent as nothing — at runtime the grant is generic, so a dispatched `product-reviewer` handed a six-item worklist spawned a second `product-reviewer` carrying the same brief, and the nested line in the agent tree read as ordinary fan-out. `browser-verifier` was the sole template with the rule already in its body, and only because someone hit the failure there first. `Explore` is the deliberate exception — nesting is its designed behaviour, so it was never missing anything. A restriction that lives in a comment is enforced against readers of the file, never against the agent.
- **Re-delegation is invisible from the dispatcher's side, which is what makes it worth a rule.** The parent reformats the child's work into its own output contract, so the report arrives correctly shaped and on time. What silently drops is the brief: a clause like *verify each premise against the code and name file:line* binds the agent you dispatched, and reaches the child only as that agent's paraphrase — so citations come back gathered under a brief you never wrote. It also manufactures corroboration, since a premise you supplied, restated by a child and relayed by a parent, arrives looking like two agents independently agreeing.
- **The sweep's own instrument had the same blind spot as the defect.** Enumerating Agent-holders with `grep '^  - Agent'` found seven templates and missed `task-builder`, which omits `tools:` entirely to receive the full set — a capability with no line to match, in the one role built to be dispatched many-in-parallel against file partitions. The same pass missed the plugin's own `.claude/agents/`, a third copy location beside the two project checkouts, easy to overlook because the plugin isn't thought of as a project. Both are patched; enumerate by capability, not by the grant's usual spelling.
- **A review verdict that the template/agent pairs matched was wrong on two of five, and the comparison took three lines.** "Word-for-word identical" is exactly what silent drift produces, so the parity section now says to run the diff rather than accept the finding. `Plan` and `code-simplifier` had each adapted the constraint to their own file's structure and drifted to different closing clauses — the template warning that a dispatcher cannot tell, the agent warning about parallel overwrites. Both true, so each side took both rather than one overwriting the other. `browser-verifier` also gained the `📖` pointer it was the only Agent-holder missing: its own equivalent rule predates this work, but a coverage sweep grepping for the reference — the check this release's own rule recommends — would have read it as unaffected.
- New `_shared/references/agent-may-not-redelegate.md`; prose constraint added to the six affected templates and their generated copies, and to `/plan-worklist` and `/done`, which spawn these agents and now say what a relayed report looks like — prevention in the agent and detection in the caller fail independently. `/haiku` gets the same fact for a different reason — it dispatches `general-purpose` (full toolset) deliberately, so the agent can spawn a copy of itself running the same skill, defeating the tier that is the skill's whole point. Framed there as a fact about the tool grant rather than a guardrail, since that file argues against padding prompts with conclusions.

`update-claude-docs` also now scans for what a session broke, not only what it added.

- **The Invalidated classification was unreachable by design.** Capture mode's routing table has four rows — New, Violation, Misplaced, Invalidated — but the scan feeding it named three signal classes, all additive: undocumented facts, violated rules, repeating patterns. Each asks what the session revealed, so the greps they produce carry the vocabulary of the *new* state, while a rule the session falsified is written in the old state's words. Those greps cannot match it, and Invalidated only ever fired by accident when a candidate fact happened to collide with a stale rule. Measured on a production host cutover: the pass ran, wrote two correct new entries, and missed ten falsified claims in one `CLAUDE.local.md` — an alias row, a paragraph calling the now-live box dormant, a DNS pin instruction that had since flipped, and six deploy recipes aimed at the retired host. The user caught all ten.
- **The sibling skill already had the answer**, which is why the fix is worded generally rather than around servers. `task-summary` §6 says outright that a claim-based grep finds nothing when only the routing changed, and to search the old file or anchor name instead. Same mechanism, opposite direction; `update-claude-docs` had no counterpart.

`/done` Step 1 gained a seventh blindness row: a finding attributed to the diff on no evidence.

- **A reviewer will pin what it finds to the slice it was handed**, because the pre-existing case sits outside what it was asked to read. Measured: a reviewer reported a scheduler ignoring SIGTERM and hard-killing on every restart, filed against the line the session had changed, at 85% confidence and with a fix direction — while stating in the same report that it had not tested whether the old command behaved the same way. It hadn't caused it: the same image hangs identically with and without the wrapper (11s direct, 10s through it), so the suggested signal-forwarding fix would have added machinery and changed nothing. The tell is a report carrying its own disclaimer while the recommendation proceeds as though the untested half were settled.
- **The control that settles it can itself be invalid, and reads as confirmation.** The first attempt here ran the pre-change command in a container with no volume mount; it died instantly on `Could not open input file: artisan` and its 0-second stop read as "the old way shut down cleanly" — the exact inverse of the truth, and more convincing than the real result. A control has to be checked for having actually run before its result counts, which is why the row names that step rather than just "run a control".

## 1.153.0

`/haiku` names why the over-specification rule keeps getting walked past.

- **The rule was already correct in two places and still didn't fire.** Both `haiku` and `explore-delegation` said to send the skill name, the target, and only session-specific facts — the reference even names `unhobble-instructions` as its example. Sharpening either would have been the wrong repair, so neither changed. What was missing is that a minimal prompt *feels* negligent, and the clauses that fill the gap are conclusions (a reporting format, which part of the last attempt was good, a list of what to avoid) rather than the guardrails they resemble.
- **Over-specifying fails silently from the dispatcher's side**, which is why prose alone couldn't fix it: the agent returns a real edit and a confident report either way, so a pass that only executed the dispatcher's outline is indistinguishable from one where the skill thought for itself. Measured across four dispatches of one skill at one target — scaffolded prompts produced 4- and 5-line edits, the bare one produced the whole-file restructure the skill exists to do. The tell is that you only find out by comparing the result against the skill's job, never by rereading the prompt.

## 1.152.0

`/read-summary` rewritten as judgement prose, with four stale facts about the doc conventions corrected. Nothing to regenerate.

- **Invoking a skill with a path argument silently rewrote the shell snippets inside it, and `condense-task-doc` was measuring docs with the wreckage.** The harness substitutes the argument text for a bare `$0` anywhere in the skill body — code fences included — so an `awk` whole-line capture became a bare path, which awk accepts as a regex rather than rejecting. Both snippets deciding that skill's main fork were affected: the per-section line counts, and the one implementing the rule that `## Next Steps` is subtracted before judging a doc against its budget. The verdict came back plausible and wrong, so a run condensed the wrong section instead of erroring. Invoking the skill bare was never affected, which is why it survived two months of use before anyone hit it. Both snippets now read lines back through shell variables; `$1`, `$2` and friends were never at risk.
- **The skill was describing conventions that no longer hold.** It told you to check a `tasks/shared/` directory that doesn't exist, treated flat `tasks/<domain>/<feature>.md` files as a live shape when none remain, and named a `## Decisions Index` heading no template produces, so a reader would grep for it and find nothing. All four corrected against the skills that actually own those conventions.
- **Following a `Merged into` stub is still right, and the first pass removed it.** New merges delete the source outright rather than leaving a stub, which made the old wording misleading. But legacy stubs still exist and `merge-task-docs` says to watch for them, so a reader landing on one needs to know to follow it. Restored with the distinction made explicit.
- **`references/claude-md-tree-walk.md` is retired**, its content folded into the read order where it applies. At 1.2KB the pointer cost more than the content, and this was the second time a rewrite severed the citation and left the file stranded.
- **`/unhobble-instructions` now checks what its own inlining broke.** Pulling a fact up out of a reference takes the pointer with it, so the file it pointed at ends up cited by nobody while every surviving pointer still resolves — nothing 404s and no diff looks wrong. The quieter version is a citation dropped while the rule's prose stays, leaving a reader told what to do and never routed to the machinery saying how. Both happened to `read-summary` in one pass.

## 1.151.0

`/done`'s infra-only mode now recognises ops scripts as infra, and calls in the simplifier when the infra is a script rather than config.

- **The mode was defined by a list of config formats, so the scripts that operate an environment fell outside it.** CI workflows, compose files, Dockerfiles and nginx config were named; `scripts/deploy-ish.sh`, a promote/rollback script and a safety-gate check were not, even though they fail the same silent way. Read literally, the list routed those sessions to full mode, which spawns a product reviewer against ops tooling that has no user journey and dilutes the one review that matters. The trigger is now the mechanism the list was standing in for — code whose failures are silent and whose blast radius is an environment, with the tell being that nothing in the test suite would fail if the file were wrong. The named formats stay as examples rather than as the boundary.
- **Skipping the simplifier is now conditional on the infra being declarative.** It remains right for compose and nginx, where there is no logic to DRY. It was wrong for shell: a promote/rollback pair whose two key lists must stay in step is exactly the duplication a simplifier catches, and in the session behind this change those lists had already drifted — 16 keys enabled against 10 disabled, leaving live credentials on a box that reported itself dormant. The reviewer caught it only because it had been prompted about that specific risk.

## 1.150.2

Fixes a silently wrong size verdict in `/condense-task-doc`. Nothing to regenerate, but if a recent condense run told you a doc was over or under budget, the number may have been wrong.

- **Passing an argument to a skill rewrites part of its own instructions.** Invoking any skill as `/skill some/path` makes the harness replace every bare `$`-zero in that skill's body with the path text before the agent reads it. Code fences do not protect it. `/condense-task-doc` measured section sizes with an `awk` that used exactly that token, so the command it ran was not the command on disk, and `awk` accepted the mangled version as a valid pattern rather than erroring. The verdict came back plausible and wrong. Thanks to @munajaf for the report (issue #22), including the diagnosis that the shipped file was fine and the corruption happened on delivery.
- **The budget check had a second bug that predates the first.** The rule is that a doc's ≤300-line target is measured with `## Next Steps` set aside, since open actionables belong in the index and shouldn't count against it. The old check subtracted everything below that heading instead of the section itself, so any doc with sections after Next Steps was undercounted, one real doc read 66 lines instead of 105. Docs where Next Steps is the last section were measured correctly, so those are the ones you can skip re-checking. Both checks are rewritten and verified against a live doc.
- **Only `$`-zero is affected.** `$1`, `$2` and the rest survive intact, so `awk -F'|' '{print $2}'` and similar are safe. The mechanic is now written down in the skill-editing reference so it doesn't come back.
- **`/skill-creator` now tells you to run `/reload-skills` before testing a new skill's trigger.** Skills are registered at session start, so invoking one you just wrote returns `Unknown skill` however correct the file is. The error names the skill rather than the registry, which reads as a bad `name:` field and sends you editing a file that was already fine.

## 1.150.1

Names the mechanically correct way to wait for a subagent, in `explore-delegation.md` and `/done`'s citing line.

- **"Never poll" said what not to do without saying what to do instead, and the obvious wrong answer is blocked by the harness.** A session following `/done`'s "waiting costs nothing" reached for `sleep 45` in Bash and got `Blocked: sleep 45 ... use Monitor with an until-loop`; neither `sleep`, `Monitor` nor `until-loop` appeared anywhere in `done/SKILL.md`. Waiting is not a call you make — you end the turn and the completion notification re-invokes you — and `Monitor` is for external state the harness can't track (a CI run, a remote queue), never for an agent it is already tracking.
- **`/done`'s pointer to that reference read as a finished instruction**, so a reader who felt complete had no question left to send them to the file that answers it. The citing line now ends on the open half (waiting means ending the turn, a `sleep` is blocked) rather than on "work on something disjoint, or don't work".

## 1.150.0

Nothing to regenerate. Changes how `/agent-setup` verifies agent files, and fixes two agent templates.

- **Agent verification was a pile of greps, and the defects that matter are shape-correct — the right words in the wrong place.** A presence check answers "does this file contain X", never "does X sit where the agent acts on it" or "does X contradict the banner three lines up". Measured this session: a full sweep across eight agents returned clean while `code-simplifier` mandated `git status --short` in its banner and ran `git diff --name-only` at step 1, a pruner's never-remove guard sat below the step that applies deletions, and a pruner measured task-doc size with a glob that makes zsh abort and report `0` — a failed measurement that reads as a healthy doc. Every check passed on all three, because a contradiction has both tokens present and a misordered rule is present too.
- **Content Validity is now stated as a read, not a grep**, in both the SKILL.md summary and the checklist, with the three surviving shapes named (contradiction, misordering, wrong argument inside a right command) and a path-resolution check added — a consolidation commit that merges layer files leaves agents citing dead paths, and a prefix-stripped `CLAUDE.md` still resolves to a real file, just the wrong one.
- **`code-reviewer` and `product-reviewer` templates now take the file list from `git status --short`.** Both described gathering changes without naming the command, which is what let a generated copy substitute `git diff --name-only` — that omits staged and untracked files, so on an already-staged session the agent reviews nothing and reports clean.
- **`product-reviewer` is now actually read-only.** Leaving `Write`/`Edit` off the `tools:` line does not block them — the harness still grants a tool that's merely omitted (the same partial-shadow quirk already documented for Explore/Plan), so the agent could edit code it was only meant to recommend on. It now sets `disallowedTools: [Write, Edit]`, which is what enforces it, and both places in the setup docs that described the omission as sufficient have been corrected.

## 1.149.1

Nothing to regenerate. Affects what `/agent-setup` and `/update-plugin` do when a generated agent and its template disagree.

- **"The template is the true source" was too strong, and it produced the wrong repair.** Both files get edited, just by different passes — a prose or unhobbling sweep rewrites the template, a session fixing live agent behaviour edits the generated copy under `.claude/agents/`. So they can each be ahead of the other on a different axis at once, and restoring wholesale in either direction throws away a real fix. Copying toward the template has a second cost: a generated file legitimately carries project-specific text that shouldn't go upstream.
- **All three places that decide this now say to read the diff and choose per hunk**, with the reasoning stated once in the shared checklist and cited from the other two. The tell that both sides were edited independently is a diff whose changes cluster into separate topics instead of one contiguous block.

## 1.149.0

Nothing to regenerate. The workflow skills you use daily read differently now; behaviour is the same except where noted below.

- **`/done`, `/ship`, `/commit`, `/task-summary`, `/update-claude-docs` and the condense skills were rewritten from rules into judgement.** These files had accumulated years of "never do X" imperatives and mechanical thresholds, each added for a real reason and each firing on cases it was never meant to catch. They now explain what to weigh and let you weigh it. Every concrete fact — exact commands, exception names, paths — was kept; only the enforcement scaffolding around them went.
- **`/ship` lost its "copy to clipboard" step during that rewrite and has it back.** If you shipped in the last few days and wondered why the release note wasn't on your clipboard, that was why.
- **Two `📖` pointers led nowhere.** `condense-task-doc` and `merge-task-docs` both pointed at a MADR-splitting section that had moved to another file, so a doc that grew past 300 lines gave you a dead link at the exact moment you needed the split guidance. Both now resolve.
- **`split-decision-tree.md` said companion files "are not gitignored."** That's true of `shared/` and wrong for `local/`, which exists precisely to hold machine-specific notes that must never be committed. Corrected, and it now points at the file owning the rest of the execution detail.
- **The `claude-md-pruner` agent's never-remove guards fired after deletion had already started** in the template that regenerates it. Moved to guard classification instead. Re-run `/agent-setup` to pick this up.

## 1.148.0

Nothing to regenerate. Affects the interval between spawning agents in `/done` (or `plan-worklist`) and their reports arriving.

- **The shadowing rule existed but never reached the skill that most needs it.** `_shared/references/explore-delegation.md` has stated it for a while, and its own header says a skill that spawns an agent and then continues must carry the constraint in its own prose rather than leave it to a pointer. `/done` Step 1 spawns up to seven agents and then walks into a detailed "verify what they could not see" agenda — and mentioned shadowing zero times. A live session dispatched three reviewers, then re-derived two facts the reviewer was already tracing; the user caught it, not the skill. The pull is specific to this shape: having the verification agenda in hand while agents run makes starting it early read as getting ahead rather than as duplicating.
- **`/done` and `plan-worklist` now state it where they dispatch**, with the tell that distinguishes it from ordinary progress — your calls mapping onto a scope you just assigned, or describing an agent as having "confirmed" something you'd already looked up. Both cite the shared reference rather than restating it, matching how `merge-task-docs` already handles the same seam.
- **Gate B's mtime test was one-sided.** It said "files predating the session drop out," which only filters work finished before you started. The case that actually came up was the opposite: a peer editing the shared plugin checkout stamped three files *hours ahead* of the reading session, so nothing "predated" and the literal test found nothing to drop. Now reads as outside the window in either direction. (The paragraph below it already mandated the diff read that settles ownership, so the mechanism held — the wording was the lossy part.)

## 1.147.2

Nothing to regenerate. Affects how you read a `haiku` report after an `unhobble-instructions` (or similar structural) dispatch.

- **A row moved intact into a companion file can still be wrong — preservation isn't correctness.** `haiku`'s verification section checked whether a rewrite distorted content during the move, but not whether the content was already stale before the move started. A CLAUDE.md gotcha row saying blog-automation code "lives on branch `feat/blog-automation`, not master" got carried verbatim into a new companion file by an `unhobble-instructions` pass — faithfully, word for word, passing every existing check — months after that code had actually merged to master. `unhobble-instructions` says outright it isn't a correctness review, so nothing in the dispatch chain was checking this.
- **`haiku` now flags time-sensitive content for a second, independent read:** does this specific claim hold right now, separate from whether it matches the pre-rewrite wording. Scoped to what actually looks dated (branch names, "not yet merged," counts, dates) — not a full fact-check of every preserved row.

## 1.147.1

Nothing to regenerate. If a condense pass recently told you a split task doc was still over budget, it may have been wrong.

- **A doc whose backlog is long no longer counts as a failed condense.** Once a task doc is split into an index plus `decisions/` files, the index has a line budget. Three skills disagreed about whether your open to-do items could be moved out of the index to meet it: `condense-task-doc` said route rows to the file that owns them, `task-summary` said every open item stays in the index so there's one list to check, and `merge-task-docs` contradicted both and itself. When the to-do list was the thing making the doc long, no legal move existed and the pass reported a miss on a doc that was already correct.

- **Open items stay in the index, and they no longer count toward the budget.** A reader asking "what's outstanding?" gets one list in one file, whatever its size. The other tables still get routed down to their theme files as before. All five files now say this the same way, so which skill you happen to invoke no longer changes the answer, and the two measurement commands the rule depends on are spelled out rather than left as a sketch.

- **One label is gone.** Split indexes used to get a heading reading "Next Steps (cross-cutting — see each decisions file for theme-specific items)". Nothing produces it now. Existing docs carrying it still work, but the parenthetical points at items that shouldn't be in those files.

Reported by @munajaf, with a reproducer precise enough to diagnose from.

## 1.147.0

Nothing to regenerate, and no action needed. This changes how wrap-ups read, from feedback that they were hard to follow.

- **When a reply needs an answer from you, it now asks at the top instead of the bottom.** Replies used to lead with everything that got finished and leave the actual question for the last line, or fold it into a table cell as a statement. You already know what you asked for, so that ordering put the only part you had to act on behind the part you could skip. A single open question now comes as a proper picker you click. Two or more come as a numbered list at the very top, before the summary. When nothing is genuinely open there's no block at all, and a question with no real "no" answer ("both changes landed, proceed?") doesn't count as one.

- **This applies to ordinary turns, not just `/done`.** The complaint that prompted it came from a normal mid-work reply, so putting the rule only in the wrap-up skills would have missed it. It now sits in `read-summary`, which most sessions run at the start, so it governs every turn that follows. `/done`, `/quick-done` and `/ship` follow the same rule for their closing summaries, and `/done` checks before sending that a gap it found actually got asked about rather than quietly marked complete.

- **Two smaller fixes found while building the above.** Changing what a skill prints now means changing its output template, not writing a sentence next to it describing the new shape — the first attempt here did the latter for two skills, and a sentence saying "put this above the table" leaves nothing to copy while the template beside it still shows the old shape. And a doc that states a count about itself (how many decisions it holds) now re-runs the command instead of adding one to the number, after an index was found claiming 33 where 36 existed.

- **The summary underneath is plainer.** Cells report what was found and stop. Phrases whose only job was to reassure ("landed precisely", "exactly the failure mode I flagged earlier") are gone, and a gap that was already raised as a question above is pointed at rather than explained a second time, so you don't have to work out whether two paragraphs describe the same thing.

## 1.146.0

Two changes to how UI work gets judged. The first came from a pasted note about progressive fetching; the second from a session that shipped an admin card reporting a money figure with nowhere to go. Three code reviewers and the product reviewer all passed it; the user spotted it immediately.

- **`uiux` now treats deferred loading as a design decision.** Lazy loading a component, a route's data or the rest of a list is usually filed as performance work, but what you defer decides what a user can find — and the trade lands hardest on mobile, where the deferred thing is below the fold and a slow connection turns "loads on demand" into "appears never". The skill now says to defer what someone goes looking for and keep resident whatever tells them what the screen is for, to check the weight is actually there before splitting (something small costs a round trip and a loading state to save bytes nobody waited on), and that deferred content still needs a placeholder at the real size or the layout shifts when it arrives.

- **`uiux` and `product-reviewer` now ask what a reader DOES with a surface, not just whether it renders correctly.** Both files already carried a version of this — `uiux` said "empty is an invitation to act", the reviewer listed "the now what? after each action" — and both got walked past, for the same two reasons. The rule only covered empty states, while the surface that failed was fully populated and accurate; and in `uiux` it sat at the tail of a paragraph about loading spinners, where nobody scanning for it would land. A surface that only reports is now its own paragraph in `uiux` and its own bullet under the reviewer's highest-value lens, with the point that correctness review structurally cannot catch this: nothing is wrong, so every other check passes. Also stated is the trap on the other side, since the obvious fix is worse than the defect — a drill-down link built on a filter the API can't actually serve looks like an answer and leads nowhere.

## 1.145.0

Nothing here needs action from you, and no output you've already generated becomes invalid. The first two items came from a session drafting a Google Chat reply into a client's thread; the rest tighten how skills point at their own reference material.

- **Several skills quietly stopped telling you things they were meant to.** A skill keeps its rarely-needed detail in a separate reference file and links to it, which works when the link leaves you with a question — but a link introduced by a complete-sounding instruction ("strip tool-output wrapper artifacts before writing") reads as the whole answer, so the file behind it never gets opened. Links like that across `condense-claude-md`, `condense-task-doc`, `task-summary`, `merge-task-docs`, `update-claude-docs`, `update-plugin`, `ship` and `notes-summary` have been rewritten to say what's actually behind them. One reference was small enough that the indirection cost more than the content, so it was folded into the six places that cited it and removed. In practice: `/ship` is now less likely to stop after committing without pushing, `/update-claude-docs` spawns its pruning agent with the git-command ban spelled out rather than referred to, and the doc-writing skills name the three style rules that need a judgement call instead of only the two you apply automatically.

- **`update-plugin` now checks that a relocated rule lands where the skill actually acts.** Moving a rule out of a reference and into the skill that needs it looks finished once the text is in the file, but a skill with both a standing "hard rules" list and its own numbered verify steps will have sessions walk the steps and never re-read the list — so a check can be present and still never fire. This bit twice while making the change above, in both directions. The skill now asks which step is running when the rule needs to apply, and says to confirm it by reading the procedure through as a session would rather than grepping for the fact.

- **Delegating a search and then running the same search yourself is now called out as the failure it is.** `read-summary` and the shared delegation guidance already said not to re-read files an agent is fetching; they now explain what it costs beyond the duplicated work. Verifying an agent's report *after* it lands is what makes delegating safe, and a fact you looked up in parallel reads as corroboration when the report repeats it, while actually confirming nothing. The half of the report you have no independent read on is the half worth checking.

- **`gchat-format` no longer prescribes the em-dash substitution that gets rejected.** Its em-dash rule mapped a prose dash to a single ASCII hyphen (`sentence - continuation`), which is the same clause break with a narrower glyph — a user asking for "no em dashes" rejects it the same way, so the skill's own prescribed fix reproduced the defect. Prose dashes now map to a comma, full stop, or colon, with a note that a space-on-both-sides dash is a clause break rather than a hyphen, and that compound modifiers (`14-day`) are untouched.
- **`gchat-format` now settles whose thread it's drafting into before shaping the message.** Nothing in the skill addressed the sender's standing among the participants, so a draft answering into a customer's internal thread came out assigning them tasks and closing out their discussion — well-formed, on-topic, and wrong to send. A new opening section names the vendor-into-client case and the posture it needs: state what the product does and what you found, leave the decision with them, offer rather than direct where a fix touches their data.

## 1.144.1

- **`update-claude-docs` and `unhobble-instructions` gained a shared routing heuristic** for deciding whether a fact belongs resident in CLAUDE.md, lazy-loaded on demand, or deferred to a companion reference — a derivability gate (can you already reconstruct it with `ls`/`grep`/`--help`?) and a residency gate (does it need to fire before every action, or only on failure?). Both skills now reason about this the same way, so routing calls stay consistent regardless of which one you're running.
- **`update-claude-docs` and `unhobble-instructions`: fixed two stale internal citations and a garbled table cell** left over from the routing-heuristic addition above. One citation pointed at the wrong bullet after an earlier edit shifted line numbers (a pattern now called out in this file's own CLAUDE.md — cite named headings, not raw line numbers). No behavior change; content-only fix.

## 1.144.0

Both fixes below came from a user hitting `/done` and `/agent-setup` in a project that wasn't a git repository. Nothing to do unless you previously hand-edited your own `Explore` agent — see the second item.

- **`/condense-task-doc` no longer reports a size verdict it couldn't actually measure.** Its "is this really bloat, or just restructuring?" check compares against the last committed copy, which doesn't exist in an unversioned project or one that hasn't had a first commit yet. It now says the check couldn't run rather than quoting a delta measured against nothing.

- **`/done` now works in projects that aren't git repos.** Previously it would fail partway through, or silently skip the code review agents entirely — the worse outcome, since it looked like it had run cleanly. It now reviews your code either way, using a file-modified-time listing in place of git where needed. One thing worth knowing: without git there's no undo, so if you're not using version control, be a little more cautious about letting agents make sweeping edits.

- **`/agent-setup` no longer fights itself when generating a fresh `Explore` agent.** Its own setup checklist and the agent template it generates from had drifted out of sync, so a newly generated `Explore` agent would fail the checklist's own check every time, no matter how many times you re-ran it. Fixed by reconciling the two — `Explore` keeps the scratchpad access it needs for large searches. If you generated agents before this fix, running `/agent-setup` again will bring them in line; nothing you built on top needs to change.

  **If you already hand-edited your own `Explore` agent to satisfy the old checklist** — removing `Write`/`Edit` and adding a `disallowedTools` block — that edit is now the out-of-date one, and `/agent-setup` won't necessarily undo it. Delete the `disallowedTools` block and let the tools stay granted. Related: the `1.60.1` entry below said both agent templates set that block explicitly. That was true when written and stopped being true shortly after, so it did not describe any release in the `1.140`–`1.143` range — worth knowing if you relied on it.

## 1.143.1

- **CLAUDE.md's own skill-table restructure broke the tool that checks CLAUDE.md's skill table.** Reorganizing the registry into "Skills by Invocation Pattern" renamed the section headers `editing-skills-checklist.md`'s registry-sync command targets (`### Skills` → three separate sub-sections, `### Typical` → `## Typical Workflow Sequences`), so its `sed` extraction silently matched nothing and reported the registry clean regardless of what was actually missing — which it was: `notes-summary` had dropped out of every CLAUDE.md table despite still being a real, user-invocable skill listed in README.md. Both fixed: the `sed` range now matches the current structure, plus a sanity floor (confirm the extracted table isn't near-empty before trusting a clean result) so the same silent self-disable can't recur on the next restructure.

## 1.143.0

- **`update-claude-docs` routed an everyday tooling default into a demand-loaded companion, where it would have fired roughly never.** The skill's Step 2 ladder resolves *scope* (subdir → layer → root → global) and stops there, but at global scope the fact still has two possible homes: the auto-loading `CLAUDE.md` or a `.claude-companions/CLAUDE-<topic>.md` hanging off it. The session picked the companion because its topic matched — the rule was shell-shaped and there was a shell companion — and the companion is symptom-indexed for readers who already have a failure in hand, so a rule about which tool to reach for on a routine command had no symptom anyone would search under. `structure.md` already noted companions don't auto-load, but only in the create/rewrite direction (splitting a block out of an over-budget file), never for Capture choosing a home for a new rule, which is the default mode and the common case. Step 2 now names the second axis: route on when the rule needs to arrive, not what it's about — routine-choice rules go in the auto-loading file, and the companion earns rules whose trigger is a nameable observed failure.

- **`condense-claude-md` would have evicted that same rule on its next pass, from the other direction.** Its companion lever selects by reference-frequency ("move the lowest-frequency rows out"), which is correct for the lookup rows it was built for — an error string or command, where the reader arrives already holding the failure. It misjudges a rule governing a routine choice, which is consulted rarely precisely because it's meant to be absorbed, and has no symptom anyone would search under until after violating it. Evicting those produces a smaller file and a rule that never fires again. Lever #2 and `structural-splits.md#7` now both gate eviction on what the reader is doing when they need the row — holding a failure, or about to act — rather than on frequency alone. `claude-md-pruner` was checked and needs no change: its lane is staleness verified against the live codebase, so it deletes rows whose referent is gone, never rows that are merely seldom read.

- **`unhobble-instructions` carried the same eviction defect in its sharpest form.** Its second orienting question — "what does a reader need on every invocation, and what twice a year?" — sent the rare half behind a `references/` pointer on read-frequency alone. That's right for a lookup and wrong for a routine-choice rule, which reads as twice-a-year precisely because it's absorbed rather than consulted; the skill most careful about companion splits silently losing content would have evicted it correctly by its own criterion. The question now distinguishes what the reader is doing when they need the row.

- **`update-plugin`'s own cross-skill sweep was conditional, so it never ran.** The instruction to grep other owners sat mid-sentence inside "if the fix touches a mechanism several skills share" — but that grep is how you learn whether a mechanism is shared, so gating it on already knowing means a session patches the one skill that misfired and reports done. This session did exactly that twice, and the two additional owners only surfaced when the user asked. It's now an unconditional first move before writing, with the pairing that matters named: skills deciding *opposite* directions on one artifact (where content lands vs. what gets evicted) are the ones a same-direction search misses, and fixing one alone gets undone on the other's next run.

## 1.142.1

- **`update-claude-docs`'s Step 1 table let a self-referential Violation get misfiled as a Behavioral rule.** A session skipped its own target file's stated one-off test before writing two entries, both later removed as not needed — the "Claude ignored existing rule → Violation" row already covered this, but its neighbour ("Claude concluded wrong → Behavioral rule") reads close enough that the ignored rule, being the skill's own instruction rather than a rule about the code under edit, got filed there instead. Misfiling into the softer category skips the Escalate-by-position guidance a real Violation gets. Both rows are now mutually exclusive: Violation names the self-referential case explicitly, Behavioral rule is scoped to reasons no existing rule already covered.

## 1.142.0

- **`haiku` dispatched a free-form audit on a criterion it invented, and the criterion was wrong.** Asked to "audit what else is not needed" against a CLAUDE.md, the dispatching session framed the prompt around obvious/narrow-scope/duplicate — a plausible-sounding bar with no basis in the request's own words — and got back a confident, well-cited report. The user's actual bar, stated one turn later, was overconstraint: instructions doing judgement's job, not entries being redundant. The skill's "Writing the prompt" section covered scope (what to include) but nothing about *choosing criteria* for a free-form task with no named skill and no stated bar — that gap is now named: route to the skill that already owns a recognizable shape (overconstraint → `unhobble-instructions`, staleness, duplication-across-files) instead of writing a free-form prompt, and where the shape is genuinely ambiguous, state the assumed bar before dispatching so a wrong guess costs a sentence instead of a full dispatch-and-discover cycle.

## 1.141.0

- **New `/uiux` skill — design judgement for UI work, including the UI work nobody labels as UI.** Covers the whole scope range: tighten one element, rethink a section, redesign a page, or survey and polish a whole module. It reads the app's existing conventions (spacing, type, component patterns) before proposing anything, so a redesigned page still matches its neighbours — and where there's no app yet, it anchors on the audience, the subject and the surface's single job instead, which is what stops a from-scratch design collapsing into a stock look. It names the AI-default looks (cream/serif/terracotta, near-black + acid accent, broadsheet hairline — adapted from the Apache-2.0 `frontend-design` skill) so a choice stays distinguishable from a default; and it covers the states a working screenshot hides — loading, empty, error, one row, overflowing text. On loading specifically: showing stale content while new data arrives reads as broken rather than pending, which is a bug report waiting to happen rather than a cosmetic issue. The skill also fires when a screenshot arrives or someone reports what they *saw* ("looks wrong", "shows the old one") without ever naming UI — a real session diagnosed an image-slider bug correctly while never mentioning that the same screenshot showed the photo overflowing its container and colliding with the title beneath it. When it spots something you didn't ask about, it answers your actual question first and then offers, rather than quietly widening scope. No action needed; invoke it directly or let it fire on a screenshot.
- **`unhobble-instructions` now covers authoring, not just auditing.** Its description said authoring was a trigger and then, a few clauses later, that "a first-draft file rarely qualifies" — so invoking it while writing a new instruction file meant reading an exclusion of the exact case in hand. The two acts are now distinguished rather than one excluded: drift accumulates after authoring, so applying the lens *during* drafting is legitimate and cheaper than fixing later. The body states what actually differs when there is no existing file — no before/after to diff, so verification measures against the file itself — and holds the same bar either way: a run ending in an outline hasn't delivered, just as an audit-shaped run ending in strategy hasn't.
- **`brainstorming` no longer claims "UI/UX work", and `skill-creator` gained the check that would have caught it.** Two review agents independently found that `brainstorming`'s trigger named UI/UX work outright while carrying a gate that blocks all implementation until you approve a direction — so "rethink the uiux for this section" had two skills claiming it with incompatible workflows, and whichever fired decided whether a spacing fix became a multi-question interview. Both descriptions now state the boundary: `brainstorming` runs first only when *what* to build is still open, `uiux` owns how a settled surface looks. The underlying gap was in skill authoring rather than either skill — the trigger pressure-test asked "would my description win" and never "does a sibling already claim this", which it can't catch by grepping your own feature's vocabulary against a sibling that names the category. `skill-creator` now says to open adjacent skills' descriptions and read them, and to fix overlap in both files.
- **`read-summary` now points at `uiux` for the visual read.** Its existing rule ("enumerate what each image is evidence *of*") treats a screenshot as evidence for a bug, which is a different act from reading it as a rendered interface — and it demonstrably missed a layout defect while doing exactly what it said. A pointer, not a second image rule, so the two don't drift apart.

## 1.140.14

- **A new checklist rule shipped with an unverified claim about which skills already followed it.** `editing-skills-checklist.md` gained a "Pointer citations" check: when a `📖` reference holds a fact with a checkable, silent-failure consequence (not just background depth), the citing skill must restate that fact inline before pointing to the reference for mechanics. `read-summary` was rewritten as the worked example, but the rule's own "Model:" line named three sibling skills as already compliant without grepping them — false for all three, plus a fourth citer the rule never named at all. `merge-task-docs`, `haiku`, `task-summary`, and `sweep-doc-overlaps` now all state the shadowing rule (don't re-read/re-grep a file while the `Explore` agent you spawned against it is still running) inline at the point they dispatch, matching `read-summary`. No action needed — this only affects how these skills read internally, not what they do when invoked.

## 1.140.13

- **A rewrite was verified entirely by grep and byte-delta, judged as damage, and reverted — destroying an improvement.** `unhobble-instructions` was dispatched at four plugin files; the first `condense-claude-md` result shed 25% and three greps for old phrasing came back empty, which got reported as systemic loss. The file was never opened. The drop was mostly redistribution (detail moved behind a `📖` pointer — the case `haiku` already warns makes a correct pass read as mass deletion), only two of the three "losses" were real, and each took one edit to patch back on a re-run. The rule existed and was walked past, so `haiku`'s wording is sharpened rather than extended: size reduction is never the failure signal, only meaning getting worse is, and no instrument in that section detects it. Added the tell — you are about to revert on evidence you could have resolved by opening the file.
- **A target was verified while its agent was still writing.** Read at 101 lines, filed "dangling cross-references", scripted the fix — every substitution missed, the agent having already repaired them in a later pass. `haiku` covered a file still being written only for *dispatch ordering*, never for verification; that gate now opens the verification section, since nothing below it is worth running on a torn page. The user's framing is kept because it names why the pull is strong: sweeping the floor while someone is still carrying bins out — real work, feels productive, void the moment it's done. Same rule added to `explore-delegation.md`, which owns the waiting-on-agents flow.
- **`explore-delegation.md` also regained a control-query technique** an unhobbling pass had generalised away (re-run a zero-result search with a query that MUST hit — an empty result is as often a broken search as a true absence), and gained a rule that a non-empty agent inventory deserves the same suspicion as an empty one, since an incomplete sweep reported as complete reads settled in a way an empty result never does.

## 1.140.12

- **Routing decided where a fact was ENCOUNTERED rather than where it is TRUE, so generic tool knowledge got buried in one project.** `update-claude-docs`' ladder ("most specific CLAUDE.md wins") is right for a fact about the project's own code and actively wrong for a fact about a framework or the harness — a session learned two Playwright API behaviours (`page.request` not carrying a localStorage token; `page.mouse` not auto-scrolling), both true in every project forever, and the ladder pushed both into one repo's CLAUDE.md. Step 2 now asks what the fact is *about* before asking where it was found, with the tell: the fact would still be true if this codebase didn't exist. `condense-claude-md` had the same gap from the other direction — its seam-test measures where symbols are *used*, which cannot tell a framework fact from a project fact, so a Playwright row with three local hits passes as "subdir-local" and gets buried; a check now sits ahead of both relocation levers.
- **The grep classification had no outcome for "my own change just made this rule false".** New / Violation / Misplaced all assume an existing rule is still correct. A project row recommending an E2E selector technique survived a session that *broke that exact technique*, because the grep matched and read as already-covered — leaving a convention that reads as settled and sends the next reader down the path just fixed. Added an **Invalidated** row. (`task-summary` already handles this well for its own artifacts — "a stale gotcha actively misleads", flip in both directions, verify before correcting — so the fix was scoped to where the gap actually was rather than copied everywhere.)
